II. 
PURPOSE AND OVERVIEW OF REBUTTAL TESTIMONY 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your rebuttal testimony? 
15 
 
The purpose of our rebuttal testimony in these proceedings is to respond to 
16 
the prepared testimony of the Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) 
17 
Finance Panel and the direct testimony of Mr. Kronauer on behalf of 
18 
Walmart Inc. regarding the appropriate return on equity (“ROE”) for 
19 
Niagara Mohawk.  Our analysis is supported by the data presented in 
20 
Exhibits__ (ROEP-1R) through (ROEP-8R). 
21 


--- Page 4 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 2 of 116 
Q. 
Please summarize your principal concerns with the Staff Finance 
1 
Panel’s recommendations. 
2 
 
Our rebuttal testimony will address the specifics of the Staff Finance Panel’s 
3 
model assumptions and proxy group in more detail, however there are three 
4 
broad areas of concern with the Staff Finance Panel’s recommendations that 
5 
are discussed in our testimony:  (1) the overall recommendation does not 
6 
meet the Comparable Return Standard established in Hope and Bluefield 
7 
when considering recent market conditions and other benchmarks such as 
8 
recently authorized ROEs; (2) the Staff Finance Panel continues to 
9 
mechanically apply a formula that was established nearly 30 years ago to 
10 
address concerns with the DCF model, even though that approach has been 
11 
increasingly ineffective over the last ten years at addressing the 
12 
shortcomings of that model; and (3) the Staff Finance Panel has failed to 
13 
consider the signals offered by the market, particularly the rating agencies, 
14 
that the outcomes of the regulatory construct in New York are insufficient 
15 
to maintain strong credit metrics and have resulted in downgrades of many 
16 
utilities operating in the state.  As shown in Figure 1 below, which will be 
17 
discussed in more detail in Section IV of our rebuttal testimony, over the 
18 
last three years, the majority of the authorized ROEs by other regulatory 
19 
agencies around the nation have been greater than 9.50 percent. 
20

4 
Q. 
What are your key conclusions and recommendations regarding the 
5 
appropriate ROE in this proceeding?   
6 
 
Our key conclusions are as follows: 
7 
• Capital markets have experienced levels of volatility not seen since 
8 
the Great Recession of 2008/09.  The Federal Reserve and Congress 
9 
have responded with unprecedented policy measures to offset the 
10 
economic effects of the COVID-19 pandemic.  The result of this 
11 
volatility and intervention is that current market data is being 
12 
influenced by external factors which require the application of 
13 
expert judgment when interpreting the results of the ROE estimation 
14 
models.   
15 
 
1 S&P Global Market Intelligence, Regulatory Research Associates rate case database.   


--- Page 6 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 4 of 116 
• In response to such conditions, rather than exercise discretion and 
1 
independent judgment, Staff continues to formulaically apply a 
2 
methodology established by the Commission almost 30 years ago 
3 
that was implemented to address the same type of concerns that exist 
4 
today; that the DCF model is not producing reasonable results.  In 
5 
doing so, Staff recommends an ROE for Niagara Mohawk that is not 
6 
comparable to the risk in the current market nor the returns 
7 
authorized for other electric and natural gas distribution utilities 
8 
across the U.S. (i.e., companies that are comparable to Niagara 
9 
Mohawk) and thus does not satisfy the requirements established by 
10 
Hope and Bluefield. 
11 
• Staff’s reliance on a proxy group that includes numerous vertically 
12 
integrated electric utility companies results in a proxy group that 
13 
does not represent the business and financial risks that Niagara 
14 
Mohawk faces as an electric and natural gas distribution company.  
15 
The flaws in Staff’s proxy group materially distort the results of its 
16 
ROE estimation models.   
17 
• Staff’s approach to setting the ROE and equity ratio for regulated 
18 
utilities has contributed to the downgrade of many New York 
19 
regulated operating companies.  
20 
• Niagara Mohawk, like several other New York utilities, has recently 
21 
experienced a credit rating downgrade.  Moody’s has subsequently 
22 
revised the Company’s outlook to negative, raising the possibility of 
23

4

5 
result in further degradation of the Company’s credit ratings.  
6

III. CAPITAL MARKET CONDITIONS AND THEIR EFFECT 
ON THE COST OF EQUITY 
Q. 
Has Staff considered the effect of economic and capital market 
7 
conditions in establishing its ROE recommendation? 
8 
 
Although Staff reviewed several factors related to capital market conditions, 
9 
Staff did not make any adjustments to its ROE recommendation to account 
10 
for capital market conditions.  With regard to the COVID-19 pandemic, 
11 
 
2 See Exhibit ___ (ROEP-1R). 


--- Page 10 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 8 of 116 
Staff argues that, although the pandemic has caused capital markets to be 
1 
“quite volatile,” that volatility does not justify a higher ROE for Niagara 
2 
Mohawk because (1) the stock market has “recovered significantly,” (2) the 
3 
effects of the COVID-19 pandemic will be addressed separately by the 
4 
Commission in Case 20-M-0266, and (3) the Company has maintained its 
5 
ability to issue debt at reasonable rates.3  Staff further contends that, 
6 
although utility Price-to-Earnings (“P/E”) ratios currently exceed their long-
7 
term historical average, that fact does not justify an addition to the results 
8 
of the DCF model.4   
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Do you agree with Staff that financial markets have recovered from the 
11 
economic effects of the COVID-19 pandemic? 
12 
 
No, we do not.  In our direct testimony, we explained that the CBOE 
13 
Volatility Index (“VIX”), which measures investors’ expectations of 
14 
volatility in the S&P 500 over the next 30 days, reached levels comparable 
15 
to the height of the Great Recession of 2008/09.5    As shown in Figure 3, 
16 
while the VIX has declined between April and November, this measure of 
17 
volatility still remains well above levels seen prior to the pandemic in 
18 
January and February 2020, averaging 16.40 during that period, versus 
19 
32.61 for the remainder of the year.  It is important to view the declines in 
20 
the VIX in the context of the unprecedented response by the Federal 
21 
 
3 Prepared Testimony of the Staff Finance Panel, at 100-102. 
4 Id., at 102-103. 
5 Direct Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel, at 22-23. 


--- Page 11 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 9 of 116 
Reserve and Congress.  As discussed in more detail in our direct testimony, 
1 
the Federal Reserve’s corporate bond-buying programs are providing 
2 
liquidity to bond markets and therefore reducing some of the uncertainty 
3 
that was driving the volatility seen in March.  There is, however, still much 
4 
uncertainty regarding the near-term effect of the pandemic on the economy 
5 
and the financial markets, which is why the VIX remains above its long-
6 
term average. 
7 
 
8 
Furthermore, as shown in Figure 3 the level of the VIX remains well above 
9 
its average level at the time of the Company’s last rate decision, suggesting 
10 
an increase in the cost of equity as compared with that time period.  
11 
Figure 3: CBOE VIX – January 2018 through November 2020 
12 
 
13

Page 10 of 116 
Q. 
Do you agree with Staff that the Dow Jones Industrial Average has 
1 
largely recovered from the February/ March 2020 market correction?  
2 
 
Yes, we do agree that the Dow Jones Industrial Average has significantly 
3 
recovered from its low on March 23, 2020; however, this is not the correct 
4 
index to rely on for conclusions regarding the performance of utility stocks.  
5 
As shown in Figure 4 below, as of November 30, 2020, the Dow Jones 
6 
Utility Average remained more than 10.00 percent below its high point of 
7 
960 on February 18, 2020, indicating that utilities have not recovered to the 
8 
extent other industries have and therefore face a higher cost of capital on a 
9 
relative basis when compared to pre-pandemic levels.  
10 
Figure 4: Dow Jones Industrial Average vs. Dow Jones Utility 
11 
Average (January 2020 – November 2020) 
12 
 
13

Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 11 of 116 
Q. 
Have rating agencies commented on the effects of current market 
1 
conditions on regulated utilities? 
2 
 
Yes.  As described in our direct testimony, Standard & Poor’s (“S&P”) 
3 
downgraded the outlook on the entire North American utilities sector, 
4 
indicating that S&P expected that the COVID-19 pandemic would create 
5 
incremental pressure and that a recession would lead to an increasing 
6 
number of downgrades and negative outlooks for utilities.6  When changing 
7 
its outlook for Niagara Mohawk to negative on June 4, 2020, Moody’s 
8 
explained that the economic effects of the pandemic have created an 
9 
unprecedented credit shock for New York utilities, stating: 
10 
New York has been amongst the worst affected regions 
11 
globally by coronavirus. The rapid spread of the 
12 
outbreak, a deteriorating global economic outlook, 
13 
falling oil prices, and asset price declines have created a 
14 
severe and extensive credit shock across many sectors, 
15 
regions and markets. The combined credit effects of 
16 
these developments are unprecedented. Electric and gas 
17 
utilities are facing depressed demand/revenues and 
18 
operational challenges in addition to political risk as 
19 
governments and regulators seek to shield consumers 
20 
from the economic fallout.7 
21 
 
22 
Q. 
What steps have the Federal Reserve and Congress taken to stabilize 
23 
financial markets and support the economy? 
24 
 
As described in our direct testimony, the Federal Reserve and Congress 
25 
have taken several measures to mitigate the economic effects of the 
26 
 
6 Standard & Poor’s Ratings Direct, “COVID-19: The Outlook for North American Regulated 
Utilities Turns Negative,” April 2, 2020. 
7 Moody’s Investors Service, Rating Action:  Moody’s Changes Outlook on Niagara Mohawk to 
Negative, Affirms Ratings, June 4, 2020, at 1. 


--- Page 14 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 12 of 116 
pandemic, including the implementation of the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and 
1 
Economic Security (“CARES”) Act, the Primary Market Corporate Credit 
2 
Facility (“PMCCF”),  the Secondary Market Corporate Credit Facility 
3 
(“SMCCF”), and the Term Asset-Back Securities Loan Facility (“TALF”), 
4 
all of which are designed to continue to support consumers, access to credit 
5 
for business and maintain greater stability in the economy.8  In November 
6 
2020, the Federal Reserve confirmed that it intends to continue its 
7 
accommodative policy over the near term to support the economy.9  
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What effect, if any, will the Federal Reserve’s accommodative 
10 
monetary policy have on long-term interest rates over the near-term? 
11 
 
The Federal Reserve has acknowledged that they will keep the federal funds 
12 
rate near zero for the near-term.  The goal of the accommodative monetary 
13 
policy is to achieve the Federal Reserve’s dual mandate of maximum 
14 
employment and stable prices.  Although the current accommodative 
15 
monetary policy will keep short-term interest rates low, it does not, 
16 
however, have a direct effect on long-term interest rates.  Long-term interest 
17 
rates can increase even though monetary policy is accommodative.  In fact, 
18 
one of the leading indicators used by investors to determine what stage of 
19 
the business cycle the economy is in is to review the yield curve which 
20 
shows the difference between long-term and short-term interest rates.  A flat 
21 
 
8 Direct Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel, at 26. 
9 Federal Reserve Press Release, November 5, 2020. 


--- Page 15 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 13 of 116 
or inverted yield curve is when long-term interest rates are equivalent to or 
1 
less than short-term interest rates and usually occurs prior to a recession.  
2 
Conversely, a steepening yield curve is when the difference between long-
3 
term interest rates and short-term interest rates is increasing and indicates 
4 
that the economy is entering a period of economic expansion following a 
5 
recession.10 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Have you reviewed the yield curve to determine investors’ expectations 
8 
regarding the economy over the near term? 
9 
 
Yes, we have.  Specifically, we calculated the difference between the yield 
10 
on the 30-year Treasury Bond and the yield on the two-year Treasury Note 
11 
from January 2018 through November 2020.  We selected the 30-year 
12 
Treasury Bond yield to represent long-term interest rates and the yield on 
13 
the two-year Treasury Note to represent short-term interest rates. As shown 
14 
in Figure 5, the yield curve has been steepening and has increased to 
15 
approximately 142 basis points as of November 30, 2020, a level not seen 
16 
since July 2017.  The steepening of the yield curve indicates that investors 
17 
expect economic growth and inflation to increase in the near term. As a 
18 
result, investors rotate out of long-term government bonds to avoid being 
19 
locked into low-interest rates for the long term.  The steep yield curve 
20

products/fixed-income-bonds/bond-yield-curve

Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel

signals that higher yields are required by investors to invest in long-term 
1 
government bonds.  
2 
Figure 5: 30-Year Treasury Bond Yield Minus 2-year Treasury Bond 
3 
Yield – July 2017 – November 202011 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
What have equity analysts said about the steepening of the yield curve? 
6 
 
Several equity analysts have noted that the yield curve is steepening and is 
7 
expected to continue to steepen into 2021, which is an indicator that the 
8 
economy is entering the early expansion phase of the business cycle.  For 
9 
example, in a recent Bloomberg article, Morgan Stanley indicated that they 
10 
expected a “V-shaped” economic recovery and therefore advised investors 
11 
to underweight government bonds and overweight equities.12  Similarly, in 
12 
a recent Bloomberg article, Goldman Sachs noted the following: 
13 
 
11 Source: Bloomberg Professional 
12 Ossinger, Joanna. “Morgan Stanley Says Go Risk-On and ‘Trust the Recovery’ in 2021.” 
Bloomberg.com, 15 Nov. 2020, www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2020-11-16/morgan-
stanley-says-go-risk-on-and-trust-the-recovery-in-2021. 


--- Page 17 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel

As the economic recovery consolidates next year, we 
1 
expect to see more differentiation across the curve, with 
2 
policymakers committing to keeping front-end rates 
3 
low, but higher expectations for real growth and 
4 
inflation driving long-end rates higher,” Goldman 
5 
strategists including Zach Pandl wrote in the report, 
6 
released Tuesday. 
7 
 
8 
This should be especially true in the U.S. due to the 
9 
Federal Reserve’s new average inflation targeting 
10 
framework, which commits the central bank to holding 
11 
off on rate hikes until inflation has reached its target and 
12 
is on track to overshoot it.13   
13 
 
14 
Finally, Barron’s noted that Citigroup also projected that the yield on the 
15 
ten-year Treasury Bond is expected to increase in 2021, which prompted 
16 
Citigroup’s recommendation to overweight equities and favor cyclical 
17 
sectors over defensive sectors, such as utilities.14 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
How has the utility sector performed historically during periods where 
20 
the yield curve is steepening, and the economy is in the early stage of 
21 
the business cycle? 
22 
 
In a recent report, Fidelity noted that the utility sector has historically been 
23 
one of the worst-performing sectors during the early phase of the business 
24 
 
13 McCormick, Liz. “Goldman Goes All-In for Steeper U.S. Yield Curves as 2021 Theme.”

cycle with a geometric average return of -10.5 percent.15  This is important 
1 
because if the utility sector underperforms over the near term, then the DCF 
2 
model, which relies on historical averages of share prices, is likely to 
3 
understate the cost of equity for Niagara Mohawk over the near term, which 
4 
is the period that the Company’s rates will be in effect.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Why do utilities historically underperform in the early stage of the 
7 
business cycle? 
8 
 
Utilities are considered a defensive sector and are therefore affected less by 
9 
changes in the business cycle relative to other market sectors because 
10 
consumers need energy during all phases of the business cycle.  Therefore, 
11 
utilities perform well during periods of uncertainty where the prospect of 
12 
slowing economic growth increases.  As Fidelity noted, historically utilities 
13 
outperform the market in the latter and recession phases of the business 
14 
cycle.16  This relationship mostly held during the past few years as the share 
15 
prices of utilities were bid up to unsustainable levels as investors responded 
16 
to economic uncertainty due to the trade war between the U.S. and China 
17 
and ultimately the COVID-19 pandemic. 
18 
 
 
15 Fidelity Investments, “The Business Cycle Approach to Equity Sector Investing,” 2020. 
16 Ibid. 


--- Page 19 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 17 of 116 
Q. 
Is Staff’s testimony that the Commission will address the effects of the 
1 
COVID-19 pandemic in a separate docket responsive to your concerns? 
2 
 
No, it is not.  The fact that the Commission will separately address the 
3 
effects of the COVID-19 pandemic on the utilities it regulates has no 
4 
bearing on the effect of the pandemic on capital markets.  As described in 
5 
both our direct testimony and rebuttal testimony, the economic effects of 
6 
the COVID-19 pandemic are currently affecting the inputs to the ROE 
7 
estimation models.  Therefore, it is not reasonable to rely upon, as Staff has, 
8 
the mechanical application of those models to determine the appropriate 
9 
ROE for Niagara Mohawk.  Rather, the interpretation of the results 
10 
produced by those ROE estimation models should reflect the application of 
11 
expert judgment.     
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Staff indicates that the Company was able to issue debt at reasonable 
14 
rates despite the pandemic.  How do you respond? 
15 
 
The Company’s ability to issue debt at reasonable rates is not reflective of 
16 
the risks faced by equity investors.  As described above, the Federal Reserve 
17 
is supporting liquidity in the corporate bond markets through the PMCCF 
18 
and the SMCCF.  These programs were initially funded at $75 billion, with 
19 
total funding up to $750 billion.  As shown in Figure 4 above, however, the 
20 
equity market for utilities has not recovered to pre-COVID-19 pandemic 
21 
levels.  This is an indication that issuing equity has a higher cost for utility 
22 
companies than it did prior to the beginning of the COVID-19 pandemic.   
23 


--- Page 20 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 18 of 116 
 
1 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff’s analysis of current utility valuations. 
2 
 
Staff analyzed the historical  P/E ratios of the Merrill Lynch Utilities Index.  
3 
Specifically, Staff compared the P/E ratio of the Merrill Lynch Utilities 
4 
Index from February 2020 to September 2020 of 17.95 to the P/E ratio of 
5 
that same index historically since 2005.  Staff concluded that, since there 
6 
have been other periods in which the Merrill Lynch Utilities Index had P/E 
7 
ratios that are comparable to 17.95, no adjustment is required to the results 
8 
of its DCF model.  
9 
      
10 
Q. 
How do you respond? 
11 
 
As shown on Exhibit ___ (SFP-20), the P/E ratio of the Merrill Lynch 
12 
Utilities Index has exceeded 17.95 only twice in the 15 years of data Staff 
13 
studied:  in 2017 and in 2019.  That is fully consistent with our testimony 
14 
that, while utility stocks have underperformed relative to the market 
15 
following the COVID-19 pandemic, utility stock valuations remain quite 
16 
high relative to their historical valuations.   
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Are Staff’s conclusions regarding capital market conditions consistent 
19

RESPONSE TO THE STAFF FINANCE PANEL 
Q. 
What is Staff’s ROE recommendation in this proceeding? 
6 
 
Staff recommends an ROE of 8.70 percent, based on a two-thirds weighting 
7 
of its DCF model result of 8.54 percent and a one-third weighting of its 
8 
average CAPM result of 9.10 percent.34 
9 
 
10

11 
it relates to the authorized ROE for the Company? 
12 
 
The primary area of disagreement between our analysis and 
13 
recommendation and that of the Staff is that Staff has dogmatically 
14 
developed the assumptions used in their ROE formula without considering 
15 
the effect of market conditions on the models, or the current financial profile 
16 
of the Company.  As a result, Staff simply runs the models and mechanically 
17 
applies the results that the model generates.  As discussed in Section III, 
18 
Staff’s model results and ultimate recommendations are not consistent with 
19 
recently authorized returns in any other jurisdiction and are inconsistent 
20 
with what the Company needs to maintain its credit ratings.  Clearly, this 
21

approach has not served well in maintaining the financial stability of the

3 
companies. 
4

11

12

13

14 
 
15

18 
our assumptions and methods are either not those used by investors or are 
19 
not reasonable.  Simply, Staff’s position is that our approach is not what 
20 
was used in the past.  Although Staff cites to many instances where it has 
21 
followed “precedent” with respect to the development of assumptions, Staff 
22 
fails to acknowledge that there are several instances where the Commission, 
23 


--- Page 38 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 36 of 116 
in fact, has changed its approach to be responsive to market conditions.  This 
1 
forms the basis of the disagreement between our ROE estimation approach 
2 
and Staff’s methodology.  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What are the key differences in the assumptions used in your analyses 
5 
and the analysis performed by Staff?  
6 
 
The specific areas of disagreement related to the calculation of the ROE 
7 
estimation models are as follows:  (1) the composition of the proxy group 
8 
and the screening criteria used to develop a risk comparable group; (2) the 
9 
weighting of the DCF and CAPM results; (3) the application of the DCF 
10 
model and the reasonableness of the results produced by the DCF model 
11 
under current market conditions; (4) the application of the CAPM and the 
12 
reasonableness of making adjustments to the inputs and assumptions used 
13 
in that model given the current market environment; (5) the level of business 
14 
and operating risk faced by the Company relative to the proxy group; and 
15 
(6) the appropriate method for determining a reasonable equity ratio for the 
16 
Company taking into consideration its business and operating risks, the 
17 
effect of the TCJA and the COVID-19 pandemic, and the capital structures 
18 
of the proxy group companies.  The following sections address each of these 
19 
areas of disagreement. 
20 
 


--- Page 39 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 37 of 116 
A. PROXY GROUP COMPOSITION  
1 
Q. 
What is your position with respect to the proxy group composition? 
2 
 
An appropriately constructed proxy group consists of companies that are 
3 
comparable in business and financial risk to the Company.  The importance 
4 
of selecting a proxy group that is similar in overall financial and business 
5 
risk to the subject company was endorsed by the United States Court of 
6 
Appeals for the District of Columbia (the “DC Circuit”) in the Petal Gas 
7 
Storage decision.  The DC Circuit acknowledged that the goal of a proxy 
8 
group is to rely on companies that possess similar risk to the subject 
9 
company for the determination of the cost of equity: 
10 
That proxy group arrangements must be risk-
11 
appropriate is the common theme in each argument.  
12 
The principle is well-established.  See Hope Natural 
13 
Gas Co., 320 U.S. at 603 (“[T]he return to the equity 
14 
owner should be commensurate with returns on 
15 
investments in other enterprises having corresponding 
16 
risks.”); CAPP I, 254 F.3d at 293 (“[A] utility must offer 
17 
a risk-adjusted expected rate of return sufficient to 
18 
attract investors.”).  The principle captures what proxy 
19 
groups do, namely, provide market-determined stock 
20 
and 
dividend 
figures 
from 
public 
companies 
21 
comparable to a target company for which those figures 
22 
are unavailable.  CAPP I, 254 F.3d at 293–94.  Market-
23 
determined stock figures reflect a company’s risk level 
24 
and, when combined with dividend values, permit 
25 
calculation of the “risk-adjusted expected rate of return 
26 
sufficient to attract investors.” 
27 
*** 
28 


--- Page 40 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 38 of 116 
What matters is that the overall proxy group 
1 
arrangement makes sense in terms of relative risk and, 
2 
even more importantly, in terms of the statutory 
3 
command to set “just and reasonable” rates, 15 U.S.C. § 
4 
717c, that are “commensurate with returns on 
5 
investments in other enterprises having corresponding 
6 
risks” and “sufficient to assure confidence in the 
7 
financial integrity of the enterprise . . . [and] maintain 
8 
its credit and . . . attract capital,” Hope Natural Gas Co., 
9 
320 U.S. at 603.35 
10 
 
11 
Consistent with the DC Circuit’s decision, we have selected a proxy group 
12 
of companies with comparable investment risk to the Company.  In contrast, 
13 
Staff applied less stringent screening criteria, which result in a larger, but 
14 
less comparable, proxy group.  
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What justification does Staff offer for relying on a proxy group 
17 
composed entirely of electric utilities? 
18 
 
Staff contends that our inclusion of gas-only utilities in the proxy group is 
19 
not appropriate because the Company and its parent are largely viewed by 
20 
investors as electric utilities.36  Staff also argues that its electric utility proxy 
21 
group should be used because the Value Line universe of electric utilities 
22 
includes many companies that provide both electric and gas utility service 
23 
and is, therefore, suitable for estimating the Company’s cost of capital.37   
24 
 
 
35Petal Gas Storage v. FERC, 496 F.3d 695, 699-700 (D.C. Cir. 2007). 
36 Prepared Testimony of the Staff Finance Panel, at 92. 
37 Id., at 79.

Q. 
Do you believe that Staff’s proxy group of only electric utilities is 
1 
appropriate for purposes of setting the authorized ROE for a 
2

3 
 
No, we do not.  As described in our direct testimony, Niagara Mohawk 
4

5 
group of electric utilities does not appropriately reflect the risks associated 
6 
with the Company’s gas distribution operations.  As shown in Exhibit ___ 
7 
(ROEP-3R), on average, the companies in Staff’s proxy group derive just 
8 
13.00 percent and 14.00 percent of their regulated operating income and 
9

10 
derives 18.00 percent of its regulated operating income from gas 
11 
distribution services and 27.00 percent of its regulated revenue from gas 
12 
distribution services.  In fact, as shown in Exhibit ___ (ROEP-3R), nine of 
13 
the companies in Staff’s proxy group (i.e., one-third of Staff’s proxy group) 
14

15 
as shown in Exhibit ___ (ROEP-1R), twelve of the 27 companies in Staff’s 
16 
proxy group (i.e., approximately 44.00 percent) are vertically integrated, 
17 
having both distribution and generation functions.  Thus, Staff’s proxy 
18 
group does not adequately reflect the risks of the Company which is both a 
19 
natural gas distribution company and an electric distribution company, but 
20 
is not in the generation business. 
21 
 


--- Page 42 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel

The business and operating risks for electric utilities and gas distribution 
1 
companies are different, especially if the electric utility owns generation 
2 
assets.  Therefore, we disagree with Staff’s opinion that the authorized ROE 
3 
for the Company should be established based on a proxy group consisting 
4 
entirely of electric utilities and excluding gas distribution utilities.  From 
5 
the perspective of investors, there would be no reason to dismiss market 
6 
data for gas distribution companies when setting the return requirements for 
7 
a combination electric and gas distribution company.  Rather, in our view, 
8 
investors would give more weight to market data for companies in the gas 
9 
distribution industry and less weight to electric utilities than is implied by 
10 
the composition of Staff’s proxy group.

Q.

1

2

3

4

5

6

7

8 
capital structure falls outside certain bounds; (2) a “Special Preferred Share” 
9

10 
and (3) a requirement for the Company to maintain an investment-grade 
11 
credit rating.46  Staff themselves concluded that “NMPC has implemented 
12 
adequate ring-fencing measures” and that those measures are “recognized 
13 
by Moody’s and S&P.”47   
14 
 
15 
The ring-fencing provisions limit the extent to which the Company is 
16 
exposed to the parent company’s risk, and therefore the Company’s ROE 
17 
should be established based on the risk profile of the Company, and not the 
18 
risk profile of the parent company.  We agree with Staff that debt investors 
19 
view the Company substantially on a stand-alone basis because it is 
20 
insulated from risks associated with National Grid plc.  For example, when

Q. 
Why is net operating income, rather than revenue, the appropriate 
15 
screening criterion to utilize for selecting a proxy group for the 
16 
Company? 
17 
 
As discussed extensively in our Direct Testimony,49 net operating income 
18 
better reflects the contribution of each business segment to the corporation’s 
19 
earnings.  For regulated utilities that collect the cost of purchased gas, fuel, 
20 
and/or power on a pass-through basis through rates, regulated revenue can 
21 
fluctuate materially with changes in weather without affecting the 
22 
 
49 Direct Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel, at 51-53.

Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 47 of 116 
corporation’s earnings or financial position.  In contrast, net operating 
1 
income, which excludes purchased commodity costs, more closely 
2 
represents the effect of each business segment on the corporation’s overall 
3 
risk profile.   
4 
 
5

Staff argues that using net operating income, rather than revenue, would 
7

9 
to risky competitive operations than the net income screening criterion.

B. APPLICATION OF THE DCF MODEL 
13 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff’s application of the DCF model. 
14 
 
Staff uses a two-stage DCF model that forecasts dividends from 2020 to 
15 
2024 using Value Line’s estimates of projected dividends in that period, and 
16 
a “sustainable growth rate” from 2024 forward.  Using the DCF 
17 
methodology, Staff calculates a mean ROE for its proxy group of 8.54 
18 
percent.  Staff applies a 2/3 weight to its DCF model results in deriving its 
19 
overall ROE recommendation. 
20 
 


--- Page 59 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 57 of 116 
Q. 
What are the differences between your application of the DCF model 
1 
and Staff’s approach? 
2 
 
The most significant difference between our application of the DCF model 
3 
and Staff’s approach is the growth rates we use in our respective analyses.  
4 
We have used a consensus of analysts’ EPS growth rates for the proxy group 
5 
companies as the near-term growth rate, and an estimate of growth in the 
6 
overall economy for the long-term growth rate.  This mitigates the 
7 
uncertainty associated with forecasting individual companies’ growth rates 
8 
over very long-time horizons.  By contrast, Staff uses dividend growth 
9 
projections from a single source (i.e., Value Line) for their near-term growth 
10 
rate, and a “sustainable growth rate” for their long-term growth rate.  Staff 
11 
states that it is “highly unlikely that investors would rely exclusively on the 
12 
earnings per share growth rate forecasts of Wall Street analysts in 
13 
determining short-term dividend projections.”64 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Do you agree with Staff’s position on earnings growth rates?  
16 
 
No, we do not.  Staff prefers dividend growth rates rather than earnings 
17 
growth rates as the near-term growth rate in their DCF analysis.  This 
18 
ignores the fact, as discussed in our Direct Testimony, that dividend growth 
19 
is fundamentally driven by earnings growth.65  While the model is, indeed, 
20 
called the “Discounted Cash Flow” model, the cash flows it refers to are 
21 
 
64 Prepared Testimony of the Staff Finance Panel, at 91. 
65 Direct Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel, at 63. 


--- Page 60 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 58 of 116 
those an investor can expect to receive during the time they own the stock.  
1 
Those cash flows are quarterly dividend payments plus any capital 
2 
appreciation that occurs between when the stock is purchased and when it 
3 
is sold.  Dividend payments and capital appreciation are both a function of 
4 
earnings per share (“EPS”), which is ultimately what determines the ROE 
5 
to the investor.  As discussed in our Direct Testimony, dividends are based 
6 
on management decisions related to cash management and other factors, 
7 
and therefore dividend growth rates are less likely to accurately reflect 
8 
investors’ growth expectations than earnings growth rates.66 
9 
 
10 
We have relied on earnings growth because earnings are the fundamental 
11 
determinant of a company’s ability to pay dividends.  As noted by Brigham 
12 
and Houston: 
13 
Growth in dividends occurs primarily as a result of 
14 
growth in earnings per share (EPS).  Earnings growth, 
15 
in turn, results from a number of factors, including (1) 
16 
inflation, (2) the amount of earnings the company 
17 
retains and invests, and (3) the rate of return the 
18 
company earns on its equity (ROE).67 
19 
 
20 
Investment analysts report predominant reliance on EPS growth 
21 
projections.  In a survey completed by 297 members of the Association for 
22 
Investment Management and Research, the majority of respondents ranked 
23 
 
66 Id., at 63-68. 
67Eugene F. Brigham and Joel F. Houston, Fundamentals of Financial Management, at 317 
(Concise Fourth Edition, Thomson South-Western, 2004). 
 


--- Page 61 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 59 of 116 
earnings as the most important variable in valuing a security (more 
1 
important than cash flow, dividends, or book value).68 
2 
 
3 
Academic research also supports the use of EPS growth estimates.  A 2002 
4 
study in the Journal of Accounting Research, examined “the valuation 
5 
performance of a comprehensive list of value drivers” and found that 
6 
“forward earnings explain stock prices remarkably well” and were generally 
7 
superior to other value drivers analyzed.69  A 2012 study from the journal 
8 
Contemporary Accounting Research found that the sell-side analysts with 
9 
the most accurate stock price targets were those whom the researchers found 
10 
to have more accurate earnings forecasts.70 
11

Q. 
Please summarize your concerns with Staff’s sustainable growth rate.  
1 
 
We have two main concerns with Staff’s sustainable growth rate.  First, 
2 
while Staff asserts that its calculation of the sustainable growth rate 
3 
represents a measure of long-term growth for the period 2024 and beyond, 
4 
the inputs to its calculation are, for the most part, shorter-term estimates for 
5 
the period 2024 and earlier.  For example, the “b * r” component of Staff’s 
6 
sustainable growth rate is derived from Value Line forecasts that only 
7 
extend through 2024.  Therefore, Staff’s long-term growth rate estimate 
8 
reflects, at best, one analyst’s forecast of only the very early years of the 
9 
second stage of Staff’s DCF model, which theoretically extends into 
10 
perpetuity.  In contrast, our estimate of long-term GDP growth reflects 
11 
projections over 30 years or more and considers overall measures of 
12 
economic growth. 78  Second, Staff’s sustainable growth rate relies on Value 
13 
Line’s estimate of each proxy company’s ROE, as the “r” in the “b * r” 
14 
component of Staff’s growth rate is the expected ROE.  This introduces an 
15 
element of circularity into Staff’s calculation that it fails to reconcile with 
16 
its results.  That is, based on Exhibit __ (SFP-9), the mean and median ROEs 
17 
assumed in Staff’s DCF calculation are 10.76 percent and 10.68 percent, 
18 
respectively, yet Staff’s analysis suggests the proxy group average ROE is 
19 
8.54 percent.  By that measure, the range of ROEs established in our 
20 
analysis for our proxy group companies (i.e., 9.80 percent to 10.60 percent) 
21 
 
78 Direct Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel, at 68-69. 
 


--- Page 67 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 65 of 116 
is quite conservative relative to the proxy group ROEs assumed in Staff’s 
1 
sustainable growth estimate.79  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Is there academic research that recognizes the shortcomings of the 
4 
sustainable growth rate approach? 
5 
 
Yes.  Dr. Roger Morin noted his concern with the use of the sustainable 
6 
growth rates in the DCF.  Specifically, Dr. Morin stated: 
7 
In summary, there are three proxies for the expected 
8 
growth component of the DCF model: historical growth 
9 
rates, analysts’ forecasts, and the sustainable growth 
10 
method.  Criteria in choosing among the three proxies 
11 
should include ease of use, ease of understanding, 
12 
theoretical and mathematical correctness, and empirical 
13 
validation.  The latter two are crucial.  The method 
14 
should be logically valid and consistent, and should 
15 
possess an adequate track record in predicting and 
16 
explaining security value.  The retention growth method 
17 
is the weakest of the three proxies on both conceptual 
18 
and empirical grounds.  The research in this area has 
19 
shown that the first two growth proxies do a better job 
20 
of explaining variations in market valuations (M/B and 
21 
P/E ratios) and are more highly correlated to measures 
22 
of value than is the retention growth proxy.80 
23 
 
24 
Considering the theoretical and empirical concerns noted by both regulators 
25 
and academics, it is reasonable to conclude, as we have, that the sustainable 
26 
growth rate should not be relied on as the estimate of the long-term growth 
27 
rate in the multi-stage DCF model. 
28 
 
29 
 
79 Id., at 110.

Page 68 of 116 
Q. 
It appears that Staff decided to rely on the mean result as opposed to 
1 
the median result of its DCF model.   Please address the consequences 
2 
of that choice. 
3 
 
Staff states that, prior to 2016, it consistently relied on the median result of 
4 
its DCF model to mitigate the influence of outlier results.85  In 2016, 
5 
however, Staff observed that the mean and median results of its DCF model 
6 
began diverging.  In response to this divergence, Staff reversed course and 
7 
began utilizing the mean, rather than the median, result of its DCF analysis. 
8 
 
9 
Generally, the mean result of an analysis is more susceptible to outlier data 
10 
points than the median result because each individual result is given equal 
11 
weight.  Divergence between the mean and median results indicates the 
12 
potential presence of outlier data points.  Therefore, while Staff has 
13 
recognized the divergence between the mean and median results of the DCF 
14 
model indicates an underlying issue with the results of the DCF model, Staff 
15 
has inappropriately assumed the solution is to change the measure of central 
16 
tendency.  In fact, the use of the median as the measure of central tendency 
17 
is reasonable and appropriate to address outliers in the data.  The more 
18 
important issue that Staff failed to recognize is the results of the DCF model 
19 
were not reflecting investors’ forward-looking expectation. Had Staff 
20 
recognized this circumstance and worked to remedy it, by adjusting the 
21 
assumptions used in the DCF models or by placing greater weight on 
22 
 
85 Id., at 43. 


--- Page 71 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 69 of 116 
alternative models such as the CAPM which can be better specified with 
1 
forward-looking inputs, it would not be necessary to change the measure of 
2 
central tendency to achieve a higher result, as Staff has done in this case. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Why have you chosen to rely on a three-stage DCF, as opposed to a two-
5 
stage DCF? 
6 
 
Our reliance on a three-stage DCF model reflects our view that growth rates 
7 
will not suddenly shift from the near-term growth rate to the long-term 
8 
growth rate, but rather will transition gradually.  While Staff argues that we 
9 
have not provided any evidence regarding the accuracy of the three-stage 
10 
model as compared to the two-stage model, Staff has not provided any 
11 
evidence on this question either.86  In fact, the evidence that Staff has 
12 
provided indicates that investors use the three-stage model.  Specifically, as 
13 
described in Section V.C of our rebuttal testimony, Staff develops its 
14 
estimate of the market return for purposes of its CAPM analysis based on 
15 
reports published by Merrill Lynch.  As Staff indicates, Merrill Lynch 
16 
develops its projected market return based on a three-stage DCF model.87 
17 
 
 
86 Id., at 88. 
87 Id., at 109. 


--- Page 72 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 70 of 116 
Q. 
What do you conclude about the results of the DCF models under 
1 
current market conditions? 
2 
 
As explained in our direct testimony and Section III above, the DCF models 
3 
are not producing reasonable results under current market conditions as a 
4 
result of unusual levels of market volatility and high utility stock valuations.  
5 
High valuations on utility shares, as noted by equity analysts, tends to result 
6 
in an underestimation of the cost of equity using the DCF model, especially 
7 
if those high valuations are not sustainable in the future.  For these reasons, 
8 
we believe it is appropriate to afford an appropriate weight to the results of 
9 
other ROE estimation methodologies.  In particular, the CAPM is more 
10 
sensitive to changes in expected market conditions, which is an important 
11 
risk factor to investors in the current financial market. 
12 
 
13 
C. APPLICATION OF THE CAPM   
14 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff’s application of the CAPM. 
15 
 
Staff developed its estimate of the risk-free rate of 1.08 percent by using a 
16 
recent average of ten-year and 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond yields.  For the 
17 
estimate of the Beta coefficient, Staff used the average Value Line Beta 
18 
coefficient for its proxy group of 0.86.  To estimate the market risk 
19 
premium, Staff subtracted its risk-free rate estimate from an average of the 
20 
forecast returns for the S&P 500 from Merrill Lynch’s August, September, 
21 
and October 2020 Quantitative Profiles.  Staff also developed a zero-Beta 
22 
CAPM using similar inputs as its traditional CAPM analysis but using a 
23 
market risk premium based on weighting the Beta coefficient times the 
24 


--- Page 73 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 71 of 116 
market risk premium by 0.75 and the market risk premium itself by 0.25.  
1 
Staff relied on the average of its two CAPM analyses and weighted that 
2 
result by one-third in the formulation of its overall ROE recommendation. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What are the primary differences between your application of the 
5 
CAPM and Staff’s application of this model? 
6 
 
Because the estimation of the ROE is a forward-looking concept, and the 
7 
ROE that is authorized in these cases will be in effect for some period in the 
8 
future, our analysis appropriately considers both the recent historical risk-
9 
free rate and the projected risk-free rate.  The analysis presented in our 
10 
Direct Testimony also relies on Bloomberg estimates of Beta coefficients 
11 
as a supplement to those published by Value Line.  Finally, we estimate the 
12 
market risk premium based on the difference between the return on large-
13 
company stocks, as measured by the S&P 500, and the yield on 30-year U.S. 
14 
Treasury Bonds. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Why is the use of 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond yields more appropriate 
17 
than relying on ten-year and 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond yields? 
18 
 
Staff’s rationale for relying on ten-year and 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond 
19 
yields in its CAPM analysis is that it is consistent with the approach the 
20 
Commission has relied on in prior cases and that the use of these securities 
21 
reflects the expectations of investors who have “both intermediate and long-
22 


--- Page 74 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 72 of 116 
term investment horizons.”88  We disagree with Staff’s rationale.  As noted 
1 
by Morningstar, “the horizon is a function of the investment, not the 
2 
investor.”  The use of the 30-year U.S. Treasury Bond yield as the risk-free 
3 
rate is consistent with the investment horizon for electric and natural gas 
4 
utility assets.  Taking Staff’s rationale to the limits, an investor who plans 
5 
to hold a position in a utility equity share for only six months would use a 
6 
certificate of deposit rate to evaluate the potential return.  That is clearly not 
7 
the case for any rational investor considering return requirements. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Why are projected Treasury Bond yields informative in the CAPM 
10 
analysis? 
11 
 
As discussed in our Direct Testimony and Section III of our Rebuttal 
12 
Testimony, recent market conditions, particularly the economic effects of 
13 
the COVID-19 pandemic, have affected the results of the ROE estimation 
14 
models.  Given these market conditions, we disagree with Staff’s position 
15 
that “current interest rates are the best indicator of future interest rates.”89  
16 
Just as investors have available to them actual and projected growth rates 
17 
for individual companies, so to do they have current and projected yields on 
18 
Treasury Bonds.   
19 
 
 
88 Id., at 54. 
89 Id., at 112.  


--- Page 75 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 73 of 116 
Furthermore, the use of projected Treasury Bond yields is consistent with 
1 
the use of a forward-looking market risk premium.  For this same reason, it 
2 
is not appropriate to rely only on 3-month average Treasury Bond yields, 
3 
when investors and the market generally are expecting interest rates to 
4 
increase from the historical lows that have recently been experienced in the 
5 
market.  This is particularly important when the expectation is that interest 
6 
rates will increase over the period that the ROE that is determined in these 
7 
cases will be in effect.  As shown in Exhibit ___ (ROEP-7R), consensus 
8 
forecasts predict interest rates to increase in the near and longer-term.  It is 
9 
reasonable to expect that investors can and do consider this information, as 
10 
they do other market projections.  Therefore, it is reasonable to rely on 
11 
expectations of market conditions to the extent that those expected 
12 
conditions differ from recent history.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Is the use of projected interest rates consistent with Staff’s testimony 
15 
on any other topics? 
16 
 
Yes, it is.  Staff’s opposition to the use of projected Treasury Bond yields 
17 
is undermined by its testimony regarding the appropriate source of inputs 
18 
such as GDP growth, the market risk premium, and Beta coefficients.  For 
19 
example, Staff stated the following regarding the appropriate approach to 
20 
projecting GDP growth: “We do not advocate a comparison with historical 
21 
growth rates because historical periods are likely not representative of the 
22 


--- Page 76 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 74 of 116 
future.”90  Regarding the market risk premium, Staff indicated that “[t]he 
1 
application of a historical market risk premium method is problematic 
2 
because ex-post MRPs are based on the premise that past performance is a 
3 
valid proxy for expectations regarding future results.”91  Finally, Staff’s 
4 
argument with respect to Beta coefficients in the CAPM was that “[t]he 
5 
problem with using historically derived betas is that, when the systematic 
6 
risks of a firm or industry change, these historically derived betas may not 
7 
be reliable indicators of future volatility.  Thus, the measurement of past 
8 
stock price movements can be a poor predictor of future stock price 
9 
changes.”92  It is not clear how Staff determined that historical data is not 
10 
representative of the future for all these other inputs to the ROE estimation 
11 
models, while simultaneously determining that the exclusive use of 
12 
historical data is appropriate for interest rates, particularly during a period 
13 
of heightened market volatility.  
14

Q. 
Please summarize Staff’s concern with your use of Bloomberg’s Beta 
1 
coefficients in the CAPM analysis.  
2 
 
Staff contends that the Commission has consistently preferred Value Line’s 
3 
Beta coefficients because they are more accurate, and they reflect the 
4 
exercise of expert judgment.  To support this position, Staff cites a study 
5 
analyzing the relative accuracy of Value Line’s Beta coefficients compared 
6 
to other sources.97 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Why is it reasonable to rely on Bloomberg’s Beta coefficients?  
9 
 
As a practical matter, the Bloomberg Beta coefficients that we have relied 
10 
on are comparable to those reported by Value Line.  Furthermore, it is 
11 
generally reasonable to consider several measures of market conditions in 
12 
estimating the ROE.  The Bloomberg Beta coefficient is widely used, and 
13 
because it is based on a ten-year period as compared to Value Line’s five-
14 
year period, it mitigates the effect of exogenous events on utility Beta 
15 
coefficients.  As discussed in our Direct Testimony, and Section III of our 
16 
Rebuttal Testimony, utility companies have been significantly affected by 
17 
multiple exogenous events, namely the TCJA and the COVID-19 
18 
pandemic.98  The academic study cited by Staff was published before either 
19 
of those exogenous events occurred.   
20

E. REGULATORY FRAMEWORK AND BUSINESS RISKS 
4 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff’s position regarding the Company’s business 
5 
risks. 
6 
 
Staff relies on credit rating agencies’ commentary on the risk of the utility 
7 
segment as a whole to support conclusions regarding the risk of Niagara 
8 
Mohawk.  Staff further suggests that the suite of regulatory mechanisms 
9 
available in New York has significantly reduced the Company’s business 
10 
risk.  Staff’s review of the Company’s business risk is, in different instances, 
11 
(1) as compared to the overall industry, and not its proxy group or (2) not 
12 
comparative at all, and therefore does not provide any evidence as to the 
13 
relative risk of the Company and the proxy group.125  Because the ROE is 
14 
estimated using the proxy group results as a surrogate for the investor 
15 
required return, it is important that any analysis of the credit supportiveness 
16 
and business risk of the Company be conducted relative to the proxy group.  
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Have you conducted any analysis of the risk of your proxy group as 
19 
compared with the Company? 
20 
 
Yes.  As discussed in our direct testimony, we examined two rankings 
21 
developed by the investment community of U.S. regulatory commissions

Q. 
Do you agree with Staff’s suggestion that the Company’s deferral 
1 
mechanisms should result in a lower ROE? 
2 
 
No, we do not.  Because the ROE is established based on the expected 
3 
returns for a proxy group of comparable companies, the analysis of business 
4 
risk is necessarily a comparative analysis.  The relevant question is not how 
5 
much of the Company’s risk has been mitigated, but rather how much risk 
6 
does the Company have relative to the proxy group.  In that regard, Staff 
7 
has provided no record evidence.  While Staff suggests that most of the 
8 
Company’s revenues and costs are subject to true-ups and deferrals, the 
9 
recovery of gas and fuel costs are a pass-through cost for nearly all utility 
10 
companies.  Fuel costs are the single largest cost for most utilities.  Because 
11 
nearly all utilities pass through purchased power and gas costs directly to 
12 
customers, there is no risk differential between the Company and the proxy 
13 
companies on the recovery of this cost.  As noted previously, Staff has not 
14 
provided any record evidence as to the level of recovery or true-up of the 
15 
Company’s remaining operating costs as compared with the peer group to 
16 
assess the Company’s relative risk profile.   
17

investor perception of the supportiveness of the New York regulatory 
11 
environment is worsening, despite the upside opportunities offered by the 
12 
earnings adjustment mechanisms that Staff identified.

20

Company d/b/a National Grid NY and KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a 
1 
National Grid: 
2 
It is important to note, however, that when the 
3

4 
year rate plan’s ROE, it recognizes the necessity of a 
5

6

7 
and business risks that are inherent when a company is 
8

9

10

11

12

13 
diminished the Companies’ flexibility and thereby 
14 
increased their level of risk. Additionally, the forecasts 
15 
of expense items in years two and three of a multi-year 
16 
rate plan are inherently more inaccurate and thus expose 
17 
a company to additional operating risk. 136

20

21

22

24

29

such as fuel cost trackers, revenue decoupling mechanisms, and the use of

3

4

13

14

15

16

17

18 
further argues that our capital structure analysis is flawed because we
19

20 
companies, rather than the corporate parents.138

Q.
How does Staff’s equity ratio recommendation differ from the 
1 
Company’s proposal? 
2 
As described in the direct testimony of the Capital Structure Panel, the 
3 
Company is proposing a 48.00 percent common equity ratio for ratemaking 
4 
purposes.  In the event this proceeding results in a multi-year rate plan via 
5 
settlement, the Company is proposing increasing the common equity ratio 
6 
to 49.00 percent and 50.00 percent in years 2 and 3 of the rate plan, 
7 
respectively.139  Although Staff argues that there are flaws in our capital 
8 
structure analysis, Staff agrees that a common equity ratio of 48.00 percent 
9 
is reasonable for ratemaking purposes.  Staff is silent with respect to the 
10 
reasonableness of increasing the Company’s common equity ratio for 
11 
ratemaking purposes over the term of a potential multi-year rate plan.  
12 
13 
Q.
Do you agree with Staff that it is appropriate to analyze the common 
14 
equity ratios of utility holding companies, rather than the common 
15 
equity ratios of the operating subsidiaries? 
16 
No, we do not.  The capital structure of a utility holding company is based 
17 
on the financing of all the regulated and non-regulated operating 
18 
subsidiaries of that holding company and is not specific to the operating 
19 
subsidiary’s operations.  As such, Staff’s analysis of holding company 
20 
capital structures includes corporate-level debt that is not part of the 
21 
regulated or financial capital structure of the operating utilities.  The 
22 
139 Direct Testimony of the Capital Structure Panel, at 7. 


--- Page 108 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 106 of 116 
relevant capital structure for comparison purposes is at the operating 
1 
company level, not the holding company level.  The Commission in this 
2 
case will be setting the capital structure for Niagara Mohawk, the operating 
3 
company, which will be used to finance investments in rate base that 
4 
provide electric and natural gas distribution services to customers.   
5 
6 
Q.
How does the Company’s proposed common equity ratio compare to 
7 
the authorized common equity ratios for other electric and gas 
8

9 
As reflected on Exhibit ___ (ROEP-4R), the average authorized common 
10 
equity ratio for the operating subsidiaries of the companies in our proxy 
11 
group was 51.64 percent and the median was 52.00 percent within a range 
12 
from 40.25 percent to 60.12 percent.   This indicates that the Company’s 
13 
proposed common equity ratio of 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent is well 
14 
within the range of authorized common equity ratios for other electric and 
15 
gas distribution utilities.  Further, this evidence indicates that Staff’s 
16 
recommended equity ratio of 48.00 percent is at the low end of the range of 
17 
allowed equity ratios for the operating subsidiaries of the proxy group 
18 
companies and well below the average authorized equity ratio. This 
19 
comparison is important because it shows that Staff’s equity ratio 
20 
recommendation would be at the low-end of a group of companies with risk 
21 
profiles that are generally comparable to the Company.

Q.
What effect does the coronavirus pandemic have on the appropriate 
1 
capital structure for the Company? 
2 
The economic effects of the COVID-19 pandemic have further pressured 
3 
already strained credit metrics across the utility industry.  As discussed 
4 
previously in Section III, S&P downgraded the outlook for the entire North 
5 
American utilities sector in April 2020, reflecting an expectation of 
6 
weakening credit measures.  S&P explained that it expects bad debt expense 
7 
to increase across the industry as customers increasingly struggle to pay 
8 
their bills.  Further, S&P anticipates lower usage from commercial and 
9 
industrial customers.140  Moody’s indicated that these issues are particularly 
10 
pronounced in New York when revising the Company’s outlook from stable 
11 
to negative.141  Increasing the Company’s equity ratio is one available lever 
12 
to alleviate these cash flow challenges. 
13 
14

15 
for the Company? 
16 
As discussed previously, the TCJA places additional pressure on utility 
17 
operating company cash flows and thus has been viewed negatively by 
18 
credit rating agencies.  All three rating agencies have commented on the 
19 
potential negative implications for utilities which include the loss of bonus

Q.

5

6

7 
Mr. Kronauer urges the Commission to consider the effect on the 
8

9

10

11 
2020. Specifically, Kronauer provides average returns in each year for all 
12 
distribution-only electric utility companies. The comparable return data 
13 
provided by Mr. Kronauer is consistent with data we used to create Figure 
14 
1, above. Mr. Kronauer notes that the proposed ROE of 9.50 percent for 
15 
Niagara Mohawk plus the proposed 50 basis point stay-out premium, which 
16 
is within the range of results presented in our direct testimony, exceeds the 
17 
ROEs awarded by this Commission from 2017 through 2020 and matches 
18 
the top end of the range of recently authorized ROEs for distribution-only 
19 
electric utilities across the country. 
20

Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 113 of 116 
Q.
What is your response to Mr. Kronauer’s testimony? 
1 
Although Mr. Kronauer refers to past returns on equity authorized by this 
2 
and other commissions, he did not conduct any analysis of market 
3 
conditions at the time that the Commission or others, authorized these 
4 
returns, nor did he compare those conditions to current market conditions. 
5 
Furthermore, Mr. Kronauer has not offered an analysis of the investor-
6 
required return on equity using a DCF, CAPM, or other market-based 
7 
model. 
8 
9 
As shown in Figure 7 in our direct testimony, the average P/E ratio for the 
10 
companies in the proxy group reached historically high levels, indicating 
11 
that the current valuations may not be sustainable.  For example, Value Line 
12 
is projecting that the P/E ratios for the companies in the proxy group will 
13 
decline from current levels over the period from 2023 through 2025. This 
14 
projected decline in utility share prices results in a corresponding increase 
15 
in the dividend yields of these utility companies causing ROE estimates 
16 
from models such as the DCF to also increase. Therefore, it is reasonable to 
17 
expect that ROE awards and investors’ return requirements will be 
18 
increasing from current levels.  
19

Rebuttal Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel

VII. CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATION
Q.
What is your conclusion regarding a fair ROE for Niagara Mohawk?
1 
Our ROE recommendation considers the results of the DCF and CAPM
2 
models, summarized in our direct testimony and modified in Figure 9 to
3 
reflect market information through November 30, 2020.  Additionally, we
4 
consider the business and financial risks of the Company as compared to
5 
the proxy group, and the effects of Federal Tax Reform and the COVID-19
6 
pandemic on the cash flow metrics of utilities.  In our view, this information
7 
suggests that the 9.50 percent ROE requested by the Company is quite
8 
conservative.
9
